    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC 1st Grade & College Lecturer</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    In <strong>1837</strong>, British army officer <strong>Captain Burt (कैप्टन बर्ट)</strong> discovered the famous minor rock edict of Emperor Ashoka at <strong>Bijak ki Pahadi (Bairath)</strong>, which explicitly proclaimed Ashoka's faith in the <strong>Triratna (Buddha, Dhamma, Sangha)</strong>. What is this edict named?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Rumminidei Pillar Edict</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Girnar Rock Edict</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Maski Rock Inscription</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Bhabru Rock Edict (भाब्रू शिलालेख - currently preserved in Asiatic Society Museum, Kolkata)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">

      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Captain Burt discovered the **Bhabru Rock Inscription (भाब्रू शिलालेख)** in 1837 on Bijak-ki-Pahadi at Bairath (Jaipur). It was chiseled out and transported to the **Asiatic Society of Bengal Museum in Kolkata** in 1840. In this inscription, Ashoka addresses the Sangha and expresses supreme reverence for the *Buddha, Dhamma, and Sangha*.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A) Rumminidei?</strong> Rumminidei Pillar (Nepal) records Ashoka's tax reduction in Lumbini (birthplace of Buddha).</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) Girnar?</strong> Girnar (Gujarat) contains Major Rock Edicts and Rudradaman's Junagadh inscription.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Maski?</strong> Maski Inscription (Karnataka) is famous for naming Ashoka directly as *Devanampiya Ashoka*.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Bhabru Edict = Discovered 1837 by Capt. Burt at Bijak ki Pahadi (Bairath) -> Kept at Kolkata Museum.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="A">

  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">

  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> 16 coins; invader: Huna king Mihirakula (हूण शासक मिहिरकुल)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> 8 coins; invader: Toramana</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> 28 coins; invader: Mahmud Ghaznavi</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> 36 coins; invader: Bakhtiyar Khilji</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(A) 16 coins; invader: Huna king Mihirakula (हूण शासक मिहिरकुल)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Excavations at Bairath yielded 36 silver coins in a clay pot, of which 8 were Indian punch-marked coins and 28 were Indo-Greek kings (with **16 coins belonging to King Menander** alone). In the 6th century AD, fanatic **Huna ruler Mihirakula** invaded and destroyed all the Buddhist monasteries and circular Chaityas at Bairath (as corroborated by Chinese pilgrim Xuanzang in 634 AD).<br><br>

          <li><strong>Why not (B)?</strong> Toramana was Mihirakula's father who fought Bhanugupta (Eran inscription).</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C)?</strong> Mahmud Ghaznavi destroyed temples in the 11th century (Gogaji/Somnath).</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D)?</strong> Bakhtiyar Khilji destroyed Nalanda and Vikramashila universities in Bihar.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Menander Coins = 16 coins at Bairath; Buddhist Monasteries destroyed by = Huna king Mihirakula; Chinese traveler = Xuanzang (Hiuen Tsang).

  </details>

Misra at the <strong>Mahasathiyan Mound (महासतियों का टीला)</strong>, has yielded the earliest archaeological evidence of <strong>Animal Husbandry (पशुपालन के प्राचीनतम साक्ष्य)</strong> in India alongside Adamgarh (MP)?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Gilund (गिलूण्ड - Rajsamand)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Bagor (बागोर, भीलवाड़ा)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Balathal (बालाथल - Udaipur)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Ojhiyana (ओझियाना - Bhilwara)</div>

      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> **Bagor (बागोर)** on the Kothari River in Bhilwara (excavated 1967–70 by V.N. Misra and L.S. Leshnik) is India's largest Mesolithic micro-lithic site. It provided the **earliest scientific evidence of cattle and sheep domestication/animal husbandry** dating back to 5000–4000 BC.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>

          <li><strong>Why not (C) Balathal?</strong> Balathal (Udaipur) yielded an 11-room fort structure, copper tools, and a 4000-year-old skeleton showing earliest leprosy affliction.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Ojhiyana?</strong> Ojhiyana (Bhilwara) is an Ahar-affiliated hill-slope site yielding distinctive white-painted terracotta bull figurines.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Earliest Animal Husbandry = Bagor (Bhilwara) & Tilwara (Barmer); 11-room fort-like structure = Balathal; White painted bull = Ojhiyana.

  </div>

    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Pulakeshin II</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Yashovarman</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Man Mori (मान मोरी - Mori king of Chittor)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">

      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Bappa Rawal (real name *Khalbhoj*) tended cows for ascetic sage **Harit Rishi**. With the sage's blessings, Bappa defeated **King Man Mori in 734 AD**, conquered Chittor, and established **Eklingji (Lord Shiva)** as the supreme sovereign deity of Mewar (with Mewar Maharanas ruling as his *Diwans*). Bappa issued a gold coin weighing 115 grains.<br><br>

          <li><strong>Why not (C) Yashovarman?</strong> Yashovarman was the king of Kannauj who patronized poet Bhavabhuti.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Bappa Rawal captured Chittor in 734 AD from = Man Mori; Guru = Harit Rishi; Supreme Deity = Eklingji; Gold coin = 115 grains.

    </div>
  </details>
</div>

    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Battle of Tarain (तराइन का युद्ध)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Battle of Singoli (सिंगोली का युद्ध)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Battle of Gagron (गागरोन का युद्ध)</div>

      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> In the **Battle of Bhutala (1227 AD)**, Rawal Jaitra Singh's brave generals **Balak and Madan** routed the forces of Sultan Iltutmish. This victory is vividly chronicled in **Jaysingh Suri's text 'Hammir Mada Mardana' (हम्मीर मद मर्दन)**. Because retreating Delhi troops burned Nagda, Jaitra Singh shifted Mewar's capital to the fortified hill-top of Chittorgarh.<br><br>

          <li><strong>Why not (C) Singoli?</strong> Battle of Singoli (1336 AD) was fought by Rana Hammir against Muhammad bin Tughlaq.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Gagron?</strong> Battle of Gagron (1519 AD) was fought by Rana Sanga against Mahmud Khilji II of Malwa.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Battle of Bhutala (1227 AD) = Jaitra Singh vs Iltutmish; Generals = Balak & Madan; Book = Hammir Mada Mardana (Jaysingh Suri); New Capital = Chittorgarh.

    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Jaita and Kumpa (जैता एवं कूंपा - 1544 Giri Sumel)</div>

      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> During the 1303 siege by Alauddin Khilji, Rawal Ratan Singh's commanders **Gora and Badal** fought heroically to the death (*Kesarriya*). **Rani Padmini** led 1,600 brave Rajput women into the historic fire *Jauhar* on 26 August 1303.         <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> 1303 Chittor heroes = Gora & Badal; 1568 Chittor heroes = Jaimal & Fatta; 1544 Giri Sumel heroes = Jaita & Kumpa; 1182 Mahoba heroes = Alha & Udal.

      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Maharana Kumbha erected the magnificent **Vijay Stambha (1440–1448 AD)** in Chittorgarh to celebrate defeating Mahmud Khilji I at Sarangpur (1437). Standing 122 feet high with 157 steps across 9 storeys, it was sculpted by master architect **Jaita** and his sons **Napa, Punja, and Poma**.           <li><strong>Why not (B) Katargarh?</strong> *Katargarh* (Eye of Mewar) is the inner citadel residence inside Kumbhalgarh Fort designed by Mandan.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Hawa Mahal?</strong> *Hawa Mahal* was built in Jaipur in 1799 by Sawai Pratap Singh.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Vijay Stambha = 9 storeys + Kumbha (Victory over Malwa 1437) + Architect Jaita; Jain Kirti Stambha = 7 storeys + Merchant Jijak (Adinath).
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="D">

    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Jauhar custom; commander: Hasan Khan Mewati</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Dawa Pratha; commander: Medini Rai</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Pati Perawan tradition (पाती पेरवण प्रथा); battlefield crown worn by: Jhala Ajja of Sadri (झाला अज्जा)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>

      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Rana Sanga revived the ancient martial tradition **'Pati Perawan' (पाती पेरवण)**, inviting all Rajput kings (including Prithviraj of Amer, Rao Ganga's son Maldeo of Marwar, Kalyamal of Bikaner, Hasan Khan Mewati, Mahmud Lodi). When Sanga was rendered unconscious by an arrow at Khanwa, **Jhala Ajja of Sadri** assumed the royal insignia and umbrella (*Chhatra*) on his elephant to continue the battle.<br><br>

          <li><strong>Why not (A)?</strong> Samanta Pratha was the internal feudal land grant system of Rajputana states.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (B)?</strong> Hasan Khan Mewati was Sanga's chief Muslim commander who fought with 12,000 horsemen and achieved martyrdom.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C)?</strong> Medini Rai was the ruler of Chanderi who fought alongside Sanga.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Pati Perawan = Rana Sanga 1527; Insignia worn at Khanwa = Jhala Ajja; Insignia worn at Haldighati = Jhala Manna (Bida); Muslim general at Khanwa = Hasan Khan Mewati; Muslim general at Haldighati = Hakim Khan Sur.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="A">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Mewar Dynasty — Maharana Pratap & Battle of Haldighati 1576 — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC 1st Grade & SI</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    Match the historic designations and titles given by various historians to the <strong>Battle of Haldighati (18 June 1576)</strong>:<br><br><strong>Which of the following pairings is INCORRECT?</strong>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Dr. A.L. Srivastava — 'Battle of Marathon' (मैराथन का युद्ध)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Abul Fazl (in Akbarnama) — 'Battle of Khamnor' (खमनौर का युद्ध)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Col. James Tod — 'Thermopylae of Mewar' (मेवाड़ की थर्मोपल्ली)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Abdul Qadir Badayuni (eye-witness) — 'Battle of Gogunda' (गोगुन्दा का युद्ध)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(A) Dr. A.L. Srivastava — 'Battle of Marathon' (मैराथन का युद्ध)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> <strong>Col. James Tod</strong> dubbed the **Battle of Dewair (October 1582)** as the <em>'Marathon of Mewar' (मेवाड़ का मैराथन)</em>, NOT Haldighati. Dr. A.L. Srivastava designated Haldighati as the *'Battle of Badshah Bagh'*.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) Abul Fazl?</strong> Abul Fazl called it the **'Battle of Khamnor'** in *Ain-i-Akbari / Akbarnama*.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Col. Tod?</strong> Col. Tod called Haldighati the **'Thermopylae of Mewar'** comparing Pratap to King Leonidas of Sparta.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Badayuni?</strong> Badayuni (who was physically present and soaked his beard in Rajput blood) called Haldighati the **'Battle of Gogunda'** in *Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh*.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Haldighati: Badayuni = Gogunda; Abul Fazl = Khamnor; Col. Tod = Thermopylae; Dewair (1582) = Marathon of Mewar (Col. Tod).
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="B">

  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">

  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Babur</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Sher Shah Suri (शेरशाह सूरी)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Humayun</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Islam Shah Suri</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(B) Sher Shah Suri (शेरशाह सूरी)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> In the Battle of Giri-Sumel (1544), though Maldeo retreated due to forged letters planted by Sher Shah, Maldeo's loyal commanders **Jaita and Kumpa** attacked Sher Shah's 80,000-strong Afghan army with just 12,000 warriors, almost wiping out the Afghan frontline before Jalal Khan Jalwani arrived with reinforcements. A relieved Sher Shah made the immortal statement about trading the empire of Hindustan for a handful of millet.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A) Babur?</strong> Babur fought Khanwa in 1527.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Humayun?</strong> Humayun had sought refuge from Maldeo in 1541 at Phalodi/Jodhpur.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Islam Shah?</strong> Islam Shah was Sher Shah's son.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> 'Handful of millet' remark = Sher Shah Suri (Battle of Giri-Sumel 1544); Heroes = Jaita & Kumpa.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="C">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Marwar Dynasty — Rao Chandrasen 'Forgotten Hero' — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC College Lecturer & 2nd Grade</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    Why is <strong>Rao Chandrasen of Marwar (1562–1581 AD)</strong> reverently remembered in Indian history as the <strong>'Agregami of Maharana Pratap' (प्रताप का अग्रगामी)</strong> and the <strong>'Forgotten Hero' (भूला-बिसरा राजा)</strong>?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Because he accepted Akbar's sovereignty at the Nagaur Darbar in 1570</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Because he surrendered Jodhpur Fort peacefully to Jahangir</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Because he was the first ruler in Rajasthan to initiate guerrilla warfare (छापामार युद्ध) against Mughal Emperor Akbar from mountain hideouts (Bhadrajun and Siwana), inspiring Maharana Pratap's later struggle</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Because he composed the famous Marwari Dingal grammar</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(C) Because he was the first ruler in Rajasthan to initiate guerrilla warfare (छापामार युद्ध) against Mughal Emperor Akbar from mountain hideouts (Bhadrajun and Siwana), inspiring Maharana Pratap's later struggle</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Rao Chandrasen (son of Rao Maldeo) attended Akbar's **Nagaur Darbar in 1570**, but observing Akbar's discriminatory pro-Mota Raja Udai Singh stance, he walked out without submitting. He spent his life in the hills of Bhadrajun, Siwana, and Sarana waging guerrilla warfare without ever surrendering to the Mughals until his death in 1581.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A)?</strong> Chandrasen refused to submit; his brothers Ram and Mota Raja Udai Singh accepted Mughal service.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (B)?</strong> Jahangir ruled much later (1605–1627).</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D)?</strong> Grammatical works were authored by poets like Hemachandra and Muraridan.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Agregami of Pratap / Bhoola Bisra Raja / Pratap of Marwar = Rao Chandrasen; Fortress sanctuary of Rathores = Siwana Fort.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="D">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Chauhans of Ajmer — Vigraharaj IV (Harkeli & Adhai Din Ka Jhonpra) — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC 1st Grade & RAS Pre</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    Which Chauhan Emperor of Ajmer, praised as <strong>'Kavi Bandhava' (कवि बान्धव)</strong>, authored the famous Sanskrit drama <strong>'Harkeli' (हरकेलि नाटक)</strong>, whose verses are inscribed on the walls of <strong>Adhai Din Ka Jhonpra</strong> and on the memorial monument of Raja Ram Mohan Roy in Bristol, England?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Ajayraj Chauhan (1113 AD)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Prithviraj Chauhan III</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Arnoraj Chauhan</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Vigraharaj IV / Visaldev (विग्रहराज चतुर्थ / बीसलदेव)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(D) Vigraharaj IV / Visaldev (विग्रहराज चतुर्थ / बीसलदेव)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> **Vigraharaj IV (1153–1163 AD)** was a scholar-king titled *Kavi Bandhava*. He authored the Sanskrit drama **Harkeli** (depicting Arjuna's battle with Lord Shiva in Kirata form) and built a grand Sanskrit College in Ajmer. In 1194, Qutbuddin Aibak converted it into a mosque (**Adhai Din Ka Jhonpra**). Portions of *Harkeli* verses are also inscribed on Raja Ram Mohan Roy's tomb in Bristol.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A) Ajayraj?</strong> Ajayraj founded Ajmer in 1113 AD and built Ajaymeru Fort (Taragarh).</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) Prithviraj III?</strong> Prithviraj III was titled *Rai Pithora* and fought the Battles of Tarain (1191/1192).</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Arnoraj?</strong> Arnoraj built Ana Sagar Lake (1137 AD) and the Varaha Temple at Pushkar.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Harkeli Drama & Kavi Bandhava = Vigraharaj IV (Visaldev); Court poet Somadeva = Lalit Vigraharaj; Court poet Narapati Nalha = Bisaldeo Raso.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="A">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Chauhans of Ranthambore — Hammir Dev & 1st Saka 1301 AD — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC EO/RO & 2nd Grade</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    During the siege of Ranthambore Fort by Alauddin Khilji in <strong>1301 AD</strong>, which two treacherous ministers betrayed <strong>Rana Hammir Dev Chauhan</strong> by revealing secret passages and polluting food stores with animal carcasses?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Rannmal and Rati Pal (रणमल एवं रतिपाल)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Bika Dahiya and Bhoja</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Jaimal and Patta</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Gora and Badal</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(A) Rannmal and Rati Pal (रणमल एवं रतिपाल)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Hammir's ministers **Rannmal and Rati Pal** defected to Alauddin Khilji after being bribed with the promise of being made rulers of Ranthambore. This forced the Rajputs into the **1st Saka of Rajasthan (11 July 1301)**, where **Queen Ranga Devi and Princess Padmala** performed Rajasthan's first recorded **Jal Jauhar (जल जौहर)** in Padmala Talab. Hammir attained martyrdom.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) Bika Dahiya?</strong> Bika Dahiya was the traitor who betrayed Kanhadade Chauhan during the **1311 Jalore Siege**.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Jaimal & Patta?</strong> Jaimal and Patta were loyal heroes of Chittor in 1568.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Gora & Badal?</strong> Gora and Badal were loyal heroes of Chittor in 1303.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Traitor of Ranthambore (1301) = Rannmal & Rati Pal; Traitor of Siwana (1308) = Bhayla Pawar; Traitor of Jalore (1311) = Bika Dahiya.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="B">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Chauhans of Jalore — Kanhadade & Battle of Siwana 1308 — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC RAS Pre & 1st Grade</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    When Sultan Alauddin Khilji captured <strong>Siwana Fort (the key to Jalore)</strong> in 1308 after the supreme sacrifice of <strong>Satalde and Som Chauhan</strong>, what new Islamic name was assigned to Siwana Fort?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Khizrabad (खिजराबाद - Chittor)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Khairabad (खैराबाद - Siwana Fort)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Jalalabad (जलालाबाद - Jalore Fort)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Mominabad (मोमिनाबाद - Amer)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(B) Khairabad (खैराबाद - Siwana Fort)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> In 1308, Alauddin captured Siwana Fort due to traitor Bhayla Pawar poisoning the *Bhandelao Talab*. Alauddin handed Siwana to **Kamaluddin Gurg** and renamed it **'Khairabad' (खैराबाद)**. When he captured Jalore in 1311, he renamed Jalore to **'Jalalabad' (जलालाबाद)**. In 1303, he renamed Chittorgarh to **'Khizrabad' (खिजराबाद)**.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A) Khizrabad?</strong> Chittorgarh was renamed *Khizrabad* in 1303 after Khizr Khan.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Jalalabad?</strong> Jalore was renamed *Jalalabad* in 1311.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Mominabad?</strong> Amer was renamed *Mominabad / Islamabad* in 1707 by Mughal Emperor Bahadur Shah I.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Siwana renamed (1308) = Khairabad; Jalore renamed (1311) = Jalalabad; Chittor renamed (1303) = Khizrabad; Amer renamed (1707) = Mominabad.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>



<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="A">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Nomenclature — Ancient Epigraphy (Vasantgarh Inscription) — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC EO/RO & College Lecturer</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    The earliest known epigraphic/inscriptional evidence containing the term <strong>'Rajasthanaditya' (राजस्थानादित्य)</strong> dated to <strong>625 AD (Vikram Samvat 682)</strong> was discovered in which historic inscription?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Vasantgarh Inscription of Chhimak Mata (बसंतगढ़ अभिलेख - Sirohi)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Bijolia Inscription (बिजौलिया अभिलेख - Bhilwara)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Ghosundi Inscription (घोसुंडी शिलालेख - Chittorgarh)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Bhabru Rock Edict (भाब्रू शिलालेख - Bairath)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(A) Vasantgarh Inscription of Chhimak Mata (बसंतगढ़ अभिलेख - Sirohi)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> The Vasantgarh Inscription (625 AD / VS 682) found at the Chhimak Mata / Kshemankari Temple in Sirohi during the reign of King Varmalat contains the earliest inscriptional reference to the term <em>'Rajasthanaditya'</em>.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) Bijolia Inscription?</strong> The Bijolia Inscription (1170 AD) in Bhilwara (Someshwar Chauhan's time) provides the genealogy of the Chauhans, mentions their *Vatsagotra Vipra* Brahmin origin, and names historical towns like *Jabalipura* and *Shakambhari*.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Ghosundi Inscription?</strong> The Ghosundi Inscription (2nd Century BCE, Nagari, Chittorgarh) by King Sarvatata is the earliest archaeological evidence of the **Bhagavata / Vaishnava cult (Ashwamedha sacrifice)** in Rajasthan.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Bhabru Inscription?</strong> The Bhabru Edict was discovered in 1837 by Captain Burt on Bijak ki Pahari (Bairath) proving Emperor Ashoka's devotion to the Buddhist Triratna (Buddha, Dhamma, Sangha).</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Rajasthanaditya = Vasantgarh (625 AD); Chauhan origin = Bijolia (1170 AD); Bhagavata cult = Ghosundi (2nd C BCE); Ashoka Buddhism = Bhabru (1837 AD).
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="B">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Nomenclature — Vedic & Epic Era References — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC 2nd Grade & SI</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    In ancient Sanskrit literature, which specific geographical name was assigned to the arid desert territory of Rajasthan by <strong>Maharishi Valmiki</strong> in the epic <strong>Ramayana</strong>?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Brahmavarta (ब्रह्मावर्त - Rigveda land between Saraswati & Drishadvati)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Marukantar (मरुकान्तार - Ramayana)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Brahmarshi Desh (ब्रह्मर्षि देश - Land of Rishis)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Raethan / Rajwara (रायथान / रजवाड़ा - Col. Tod 1829)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(B) Marukantar (मरुकान्तार - Ramayana)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Maharishi Valmiki in the Ramayana explicitly designated the dry western desert landscape of Rajasthan as <strong>'Marukantar' (मरुकान्तार)</strong>.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A) Brahmavarta?</strong> *Brahmavarta* was the sacred land between the holy rivers Saraswati and Drishadvati in the Rigvedic era.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Brahmarshi Desh?</strong> *Brahmarshi Desh* was the territory of the holy sages comprising the Matsya, Surasena, and Kuru regions.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Raethan / Rajwara?</strong> *Raethan* and *Rajwara* were English coinages popularized in 1829 by British political agent Col. James Tod in *'Annals and Antiquities of Rajasthan'*.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Ramayana = Marukantar; Rigveda = Brahmavarta; Col. Tod (1829) = Rajasthan / Raethan / Rajwara; George Thomas (1800) = Rajpootana.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="C">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Ancient Janapadas — Matsya Janapada — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC College Lecturer & RAS</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    What was the capital of the ancient <strong>Matsya Janapada (मत्स्य जनपद)</strong> encompassing modern Jaipur, Alwar, and Bharatpur, where the Pandavas spent their one-year period of incognito exile (अज्ञातवास)?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Madhyamika / Nagari (मध्यमिका / नगरी - Sivi Janapada)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Mathura (मथुरा - Shoorsen Janapada)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Viratnagar / Bairath (विराटनगर / बैराठ - Matsya Janapada)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Ahichhatrapur (अहिच्छत्रपुर - Jangal Desh)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(C) Viratnagar / Bairath (विराटनगर / बैराठ - Matsya Janapada)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> The capital of Matsya Janapada was **Viratnagar (modern Bairath, Jaipur)** founded by King Virata. The Mahabharata records that the 5 Pandava brothers and Draupadi lived disguised in King Virata's court during their final year of exile.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A) Madhyamika?</strong> Madhyamika (Nagari near Chittorgarh) was the capital of the **Sivi Janapada (शिवि जनपद)**.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) Mathura?</strong> Mathura was the capital of the **Shoorsen Janapada (शूरसेन जनपद)** comprising Braj, Bharatpur, Karauli, and Dholpur (associated with Lord Krishna and Yadavs).</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Ahichhatrapur?</strong> Ahichhatrapur (modern Nagaur) was the capital of **Jangal Desh (जांगल देश)** ruled by the early Chauhans and Rathores.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Matsya = Viratnagar; Sivi = Madhyamika/Nagari; Shoorsen = Mathura; Jangal Desh = Ahichhatrapur (Nagaur); Malwa = Nagar (Tonk).
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="D">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Prehistoric Sites — Kalibangan (Etymology & Meaning) — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC EO/RO & Patwari</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    The word <strong>'Kalibangan' (कालीबंगा)</strong> literally translates to which of the following in the local Rajasthani / Punjabi dialect due to the massive discovery of terracotta bangles at the mound?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Mound of the Dead (मृतकों का टीला - Mohenjodaro)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Mound of Dust (धूलकोट - Ahar)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> City of Copper Smelting (ताम्रवती नगरी - Ahar)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Black Bangles (काली चूड़ियां)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(D) Black Bangles (काली चूड़ियां)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> In the Punjabi / Bagri dialect, <em>'Kali'</em> means Black and <em>'Bangan' / 'Wangan'</em> means Bangles. The site was named Kalibangan because excavators found thousands of fragments of black-surfaced terracotta and clay bangles across the excavation mounds.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A) Mound of the Dead?</strong> *Mound of the Dead* (मूर्दों का टीला) is the Sindhi literal translation of **Mohenjo-daro** in the Indus Valley.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) Mound of Dust?</strong> *Dhoolkot* (धूलकोट - Dust Mound) is the local colloquial name of **Ahar** in Udaipur.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Tamravati Nagari?</strong> *Tamravati Nagari* (ताम्रवती नगरी - City of Copper) is the ancient epigraphic name of **Ahar**.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Kalibangan = Black Bangles; Mohenjodaro = Mound of Dead; Ahar = Dhoolkot / Tamravati Nagari; Ganeshwar = Mother of Copper Civilizations.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="A">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Prehistoric Sites — Kalibangan (Geographical Location & River) — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC 1st Grade & SI</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    On the left bank of which dried ancient river course in <strong>Hanumangarh district</strong> is the Harappan archaeological site of <strong>Kalibangan</strong> situated?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Ghaggar River / Ancient Saraswati-Drishadvati (घग्घर नदी - Hanumangarh)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Kantali River (कांतली नदी - Ganeshwar & Sunari)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Berach / Ayad River (बेड़च नदी - Ahar site)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Kothari River (कोठारी नदी - Bagor site)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(A) Ghaggar River / Ancient Saraswati-Drishadvati (घग्घर नदी - Hanumangarh)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Kalibangan is located in Hanumangarh district on the left bank of the seasonal **Ghaggar River (घग्घर)**, which flows along the paleochannel of the ancient Vedic Saraswati and Drishadvati rivers.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) Kantali?</strong> The Kantali river in the Shekhawati / Neem Ka Thana region feeds the sites of **Ganeshwar** and **Sunari**.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Berach / Ayad?</strong> The Berach (Ayad) river in Udaipur is the location of the Chalcolithic site of **Ahar (Dhoolkot)**.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Kothari?</strong> The Kothari river in Bhilwara is the location of the Mesolithic site of **Bagor**.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Kalibangan = Ghaggar (Hanumangarh); Ahar = Berach (Udaipur); Ganeshwar = Kantali (Neem Ka Thana); Bagor = Kothari (Bhilwara); Bairath = Banganga (Jaipur).
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="B">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Prehistoric Sites — Kalibangan (Excavation & Archeologists) — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC College Lecturer & RAS Pre</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    Who originally <strong>identified/discovered</strong> the prehistoric site of Kalibangan in <strong>1952</strong>, and who led the <strong>large-scale systematic excavations between 1961 and 1969</strong>?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Discovered by Akshay Kirti Vyas; excavated by H.D. Sankhalia</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Discovered by Amalanand Ghosh (1952); excavated by B.B. Lal and B.K. Thapar (1961–1969)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Discovered by R.C. Agrawala; excavated by Dr. V.N. Misra</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Discovered by Dayaram Sahni; excavated by Nilratna Banerjee</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(B) Discovered by Amalanand Ghosh (1952); excavated by B.B. Lal and B.K. Thapar (1961–1969)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Kalibangan was initially discovered and surveyed by archaeologist **Amalanand Ghosh in 1952**. Extensive, multi-level scientific excavations were carried out from 1961 to 1969 under the leadership of **B.B. Lal (Braj Basi Lal)** and **B.K. Thapar (Bal Krishen Thapar)** along with M.D. Khare.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A)?</strong> Akshay Kirti Vyas discovered **Ahar** in 1953, which was later extensively excavated by Prof. H.D. Sankhalia of Deccan College in 1961–62.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C)?</strong> R.C. Agrawala discovered **Ganeshwar** (1977), while Dr. V.N. Misra excavated **Bagor** (1967) and **Balathal** (1993).</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D)?</strong> Dayaram Sahni excavated **Bairath (Jaipur)** in 1936–37, followed by Nilratna Banerjee and K.N. Dixit in 1962.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Kalibangan = A. Ghosh (1952) + B.B. Lal & B.K. Thapar (1961-69); Ahar = A.K. Vyas (1953) + H.D. Sankhalia; Ganeshwar = R.C. Agrawala; Bairath = Dayaram Sahni.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="C">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Prehistoric Sites — Kalibangan (Stratification & Cultural Levels) — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC Assistant Professor & RAS</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    Archaeological excavation at Kalibangan revealed a sequence of <strong>how many distinct cultural levels/strata (सांस्कृतिक स्तर)</strong> representing both Pre-Harappan and Mature Harappan phases?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> 3 Cultural Levels</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> 7 Cultural Levels</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> 5 Cultural Levels (Levels 1–2: Pre-Harappan; Levels 3–5: Mature Harappan)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> 9 Cultural Levels</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(C) 5 Cultural Levels (Levels 1–2: Pre-Harappan; Levels 3–5: Mature Harappan)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Excavations at Kalibangan uncovered **5 distinct cultural strata (पांच स्तर)**. Levels I and II belong to the **Pre-Harappan (प्राक्-हड़प्पा)** culture, while Levels III, IV, and V represent the fully developed **Mature Harappan (विकसित हड़प्पा)** urban civilization.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A) 3 Levels?</strong> Ahar shows 3 main Chalcolithic sub-phases, but Kalibangan strictly exhibits 5 vertical stratification levels.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) 7 Levels?</strong> 7 refers to the number of **Fire Altars (सात अग्नि वेदिकाएं)** discovered in a single row on the citadel platform at Kalibangan, not the stratigraphic layers.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) 9 Levels?</strong> 9 is the number of strings on the *Ravanhattha* musical instrument.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Kalibangan Stratification = 5 Levels (1-2 Pre-Harappan, 3-5 Mature Harappan); Fire Altars = 7; Holes in Trepanned Skull = 6.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="D">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Prehistoric Sites — Kalibangan (Dual-Cropped Ploughed Field) — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC EO/RO & 1st Grade</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    The world's earliest criss-cross ploughed agricultural field discovered at Kalibangan yielded conclusive evidence of the <strong>simultaneous cultivation of which two agricultural crops</strong>?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Wheat and Barley (गेहूं एवं जौ)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Cotton and Jowar (कपास एवं ज्वार)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Rice and Sugarcane (चावल एवं गन्ना)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Mustard and Gram (सरसों एवं चना)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(D) Mustard and Gram (सरसों एवं चना)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> The ploughed field at Kalibangan features intersecting furrows: wide-spaced furrows running North-South (for taller crops like **Mustard / सरसों**) and narrow-spaced furrows running East-West (for shorter crops like **Gram / चना**), proving simultaneous dual-cropping outside the settlement walls.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A) Wheat and Barley?</strong> Wheat and barley were staple cereals consumed across the Indus Valley (found at Harappa and Mohenjodaro), but the specific dual-furrow field pattern at Kalibangan proved Mustard and Gram.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) Cotton and Jowar?</strong> Cotton was grown at Mehrgarh (*Sindon*), but the Kalibangan furrow measurement matches mustard-gram farming.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Rice and Sugarcane?</strong> Rice husks were uniquely found at Lothal and Rangpur (Gujarat); sugarcane was not a prominent Harappan staple.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Kalibangan ploughed field = Mustard (सरसों) + Gram (चना); Location = South-East outside the fortified citadel.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>

      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> A row of **7 contiguous fire altars (सात हवन कुण्ड)** made of clay bricks was unearthed atop an elevated platform in the citadel at Kalibangan. Inside the altars, archaeologists found ash, charcoal, terracotta cakes, and bovine/animal bones, confirming community fire offerings and sacrificial rituals.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) Great Bath?</strong> The Great Bath (विशाल स्नानागार) with gypsum/bitumen lining was found at **Mohenjo-daro (Sindh)**, not Kalibangan.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Stone Temple?</strong> No structural idol temples existed in the Harappan civilization; the earliest circular brick temple in Rajasthan is the Buddhist Chaitya at **Bairath (Jaipur)**.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Dockyard?</strong> The tidal dockyard (गोदीवाड़ा) was discovered at **Lothal (Gujarat)** on the Bhogava river.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> 7 Fire Altars = Kalibangan & Lothal; Great Bath = Mohenjodaro; Dockyard = Lothal; Circular Temple = Bairath.

  </details>
</div>

    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Smallpox Vaccination (चेचक का टीका)</div>

      <div class="pyq-explanation">

Microscopic examination revealed natural bone tissue regeneration around the edges, proving that the patient survived a surgical craniotomy (trepanation) to relieve intracranial pressure (Hydrocephalus), making it the **earliest evidence of brain surgery in human history**.<br><br>

          <li><strong>Why not (C) Vaccination?</strong> Vaccination was invented in 1796 by Edward Jenner.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Plaster casting?</strong> Plaster casting is a modern orthopedic technique.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Child skull with 6 holes = Kalibangan (World's earliest Brain Surgery / Trepanation); Leprosy skeleton (4000 yrs old) = Balathal (Udaipur).

    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Glacial moraine boulders crushing the citadel gates</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(C) Deep fault-line fractures, wall fissures, and displaced brick alignments in Pre-Harappan stratum II</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Excavators Lal and Thapar discovered dramatic structural ruptures, tilted walls, and deep fault cleavages in the mud-brick houses of Stratum II at Kalibangan. This tectonic disruption abruptly ended the Pre-Harappan phase, providing the **world's oldest archaeological evidence of an earthquake**.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A)?</strong> Rajasthan's plains have no active volcanic activity; Harappan decline was related to river desiccation and seismic shifts.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (B)?</strong> Tsunami silt is found only in coastal ports like Dholavira or Prabhas Patan, not inland Ghaggar desert basins.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D)?</strong> Glacial moraines are high Himalayan geological features.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Earliest Earthquake = Kalibangan; Earliest Animal Husbandry = Bagor; Earliest Copper Smelting = Ahar; Earliest Iron Furnaces = Sunari.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="D">

    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC 2nd Grade & Patwari</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    Why is Kalibangan famously referred to by historians and archaeologists as a <strong>'Poor Man’s Settlement' (दीन-हीन बस्ती / Deen-Heen Basti)</strong>?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Because no gold or silver ornaments were ever discovered at the site</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Because the citadel lacked protective fortification walls</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Because the residents possessed no domestic cattle or agriculture</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Because the houses were constructed predominantly using sun-dried mud bricks (कच्ची ईंटें) rather than kiln-burnt baked bricks</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(D) Because the houses were constructed predominantly using sun-dried mud bricks (कच्ची ईंटें) rather than kiln-burnt baked bricks</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Unlike Harappa and Mohenjo-daro where houses were built with expensive fire-baked bricks (*Pakki Intein*), the residential dwellings at Kalibangan were built using sun-dried mud bricks (*Kacchi Intein*) with standardized ratio **4:2:1** (size $30 	imes 15 	imes 7.5	ext{ cm}$), prompting archaeologists to designate it a *'Deen-Heen Basti'*.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A)?</strong> Gold and semi-precious beads (agate, jasper, steatite, carnelian) were found in substantial quantities at Kalibangan.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (B)?</strong> Kalibangan was heavily fortified with both upper citadel (*Garhi*) and lower town fortification walls.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C)?</strong> Kalibangan had an advanced agrarian economy cultivating mustard, gram, and rearing cattle and camels.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Deen-Heen Basti = Kalibangan (Sun-baked mud bricks in 4:2:1 ratio); Baked bricks in Chalcolithic = Gilund (Rajsamand).
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>

    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Carved stone water reservoirs and rock-cut dams (Dholavira)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Underground glazed terracotta sewage pipes connected to soak pits</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Lead and bronze plumbing pipes with turncock faucets</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(A) Drainage pipes crafted from scooped-out hollowed tree trunks / wood (लकड़ी की नालियां)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Kalibangan is the **only Indus Valley site where domestic wastewater was drained using hollowed-out wooden logs/timber pipes (लकड़ी की नालियां)** leading into street soakage jars (*Soak-pits*), along with cylindrical Mesopotamian seals and camel bones.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) Rock-cut Reservoirs?</strong> Massive stone reservoirs and sophisticated storm-water harvesting systems were found at **Dholavira (Kutch, Gujarat)**.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Baked brick drains?</strong> Standard fire-baked brick drains were ubiquitous in Mohenjo-daro and Harappa.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D)?</strong> Metal plumbing pipes did not exist in the Bronze Age.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Wooden drains + Mesopotamian cylindrical seal + Camel bones = Kalibangan; Stone reservoirs = Dholavira; 3075 Punch-marked coins hoard = Redh (Tonk).
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="B">

  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    What is the traditional local name of the 500-meter-long archaeological mound of <strong>Ahar (Udaipur)</strong>, and what ancient title was inscribed in 10th–11th century copper plates denoting its rich metallurgy?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Local name: Mahasatiyan; Ancient name: Viratnagar</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Local name: Dhoolkot (धूलकोट - Mound of Dust); Ancient name: Tamravati Nagari (ताम्रवती नगरी / Aghatpur)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Local name: Bijak ki Pahari; Ancient name: Madhyamika</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Local name: Chidiyatook; Ancient name: Ahichhatrapur</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(B) Local name: Dhoolkot (धूलकोट - Mound of Dust); Ancient name: Tamravati Nagari (ताम्रवती नगरी / Aghatpur)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Locals in Udaipur call the great mound of Ahar **'Dhoolkot' (धूलकोट - Dust/Ash Mound)**. In ancient inscriptions from the 10th–11th centuries, it was officially designated as **'Tamravati Nagari' (ताम्रवती नगरी)** and **'Aghatpur' / 'Aghat Durg' (आघाटपुर)** due to its massive copper-smelting workshops.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A) Mahasatiyan?</strong> *Mahasatiyan* (महासतियां) is the ancient mound at **Bagor (Bhilwara)** where Stone Age tools were discovered.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) Bijak ki Pahari?</strong> *Bijak ki Pahari* is the Buddhist hill at **Bairath (Jaipur)**, while *Madhyamika* is ancient Nagari (Chittor).</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Chidiyatook?</strong> *Chidiyatook* is the hill of Mehrangarh Fort in Jodhpur; *Ahichhatrapur* is Nagaur.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Ahar = Dhoolkot & Tamravati Nagari (Aghatpur); Bagor = Mahasatiyan; Bairath = Bijak ki Pahari; Nagari = Madhyamika.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>

    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Copper smelting furnaces (तांबा गलाने की भट्टी), melting crucibles, 6 Greek coins with God Apollo, and 'Banasian Bull' terracotta figurines</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> 7 fire altars and wooden drainage pipes</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(C) Copper smelting furnaces (तांबा गलाने की भट्टी), melting crucibles, 6 Greek coins with God Apollo, and 'Banasian Bull' terracotta figurines</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Excavations at Ahar (Berach river) revealed round earthen furnaces for smelting raw copper ore (*Tambavati*), graphite crucibles, flat copper axes, terracotta humped bulls (*'Banasian Bull'*), Black-and-Red Ware (BRW) pottery with white geometric paintings, and 6 copper coins bearing the effigy of Greek God Apollo holding a bow and quiver.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>

          <li><strong>Why not (A)?</strong> Circular Buddhist Chaitya and Menander's silver coins were discovered at **Bairath (Jaipur)**.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (B)?</strong> 99% pure copper fishhooks and arrowheads were found at **Ganeshwar (Kantali river, Neem Ka Thana)**.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D)?</strong> 7 fire altars and wooden drainage pipes were found at **Kalibangan (Hanumangarh)**.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Ahar = Copper smelting furnaces, Banasian Bull, Apollo coins; Ganeshwar = 99% pure copper fishhooks; Bairath = Greek coins of Menander + Buddhist Chaitya; Kalibangan = 7 Fire altars.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="D">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Prehistoric Sites — Ganeshwar (Copper Age Archeology) — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC 1st Grade & EO/RO</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    Why is <strong>Ganeshwar (गणेश्वर, नीम का थाना / सीकर)</strong> on the Kantali river venerated as the <strong>'Mother of Copper Age Civilizations' (ताम्रयुगीन सभ्यताओं की जननी)</strong>?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Because it supplied iron swords to the Mauryan army</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> Because it has the world's largest stone stepwell</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> Because it was the capital of Emperor Ashoka</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Because its copper artifacts dating to ~2800 BCE possess an unprecedented 99% purity and it was the primary source of copper tools supplied to Harappa and Kalibangan</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(D) Because its copper artifacts dating to ~2800 BCE possess an unprecedented 99% purity and it was the primary source of copper tools supplied to Harappa and Kalibangan</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Ganeshwar (excavated by R.C. Agrawala in 1977 on Kantali river) dates to **2800 BCE**, making it the oldest copper-using culture in India. Spectrographic analysis proved its arrowheads, spearheads, bangles, and fishhooks (*Machhli pakadne ka kanta*) were made of **99% pure copper** mined from nearby Khetri deposits and exported to Harappan centers.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A)?</strong> Iron metallurgy developed at **Sunari (Jhunjhunu)** and **Jodhpura (Jaipur)**, not Ganeshwar (which is pure Chalcolithic).</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (B)?</strong> The largest stone stepwell is **Chand Baori (Abhaneri, Dausa)**.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C)?</strong> Pataliputra was Ashoka's capital; Bairath in Rajasthan contained his rock edicts.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Ganeshwar = Mother of Copper Civilizations (99% pure copper fishhooks, 2800 BCE); Sunari = Iron furnaces; Ahar = Copper smelting furnaces.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="A">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Prehistoric Sites — Bairath / Viratnagar (Coins & Buddhism) — Deep Fact Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC Assistant Professor & RAS Pre</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    During the 1936 excavations at Bairath by <strong>Dayaram Sahni</strong>, a hoard of <strong>36 silver coins wrapped in a cotton cloth</strong> was discovered inside a terracotta jar. How many of these were coins of the <strong>Indo-Greek King Menander (मिलिंद)</strong>?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> 16 Coins (out of 28 Indo-Greek coins, 16 belonged to King Menander)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> 8 Coins</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> 24 Coins</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> 36 Coins</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(A) 16 Coins (out of 28 Indo-Greek coins, 16 belonged to King Menander)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> The 36 silver coins found at Bairath comprised **8 punch-marked silver coins (आहत सिक्के)** and **28 Indo-Greek / Bactrian coins**, of which **16 coins belonged specifically to King Menander (राजा मिनांडर / मिलिंद)**, proving flourishing trade with the Indo-Greek realm.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (B) 8 Coins?</strong> 8 was the exact number of ancient indigenous **punch-marked (आहत) silver coins** in the hoard.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) 24 Coins?</strong> 24 is the number of Tirthankaras in Jainism.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) 36 Coins?</strong> 36 was the **total number of coins in the entire jar**.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> Bairath Coin Hoard: Total = 36; Punch-marked = 8; Indo-Greek = 28; King Menander's coins = 16.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>



<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="B">
  <div class="pyq-header">
    <span class="pyq-exam-badge practice-badge">🎯 Ancient Geography & Nomenclature — Deep Concept Check</span>
    <span class="pyq-year">RPSC EO/RO & RAS Special</span>
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-question">
    Who among the following scholars was the first to use the name <strong>'Rajpootana' (राजपूताना)</strong> for the entire geographical territory of modern Rajasthan in <strong>1800 AD</strong>?
  </div>
  <div class="pyq-options">
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="A"><strong>(A)</strong> Col. James Tod (कर्नल जेम्स टॉड)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="B"><strong>(B)</strong> George Thomas (जॉर्ज थॉमस)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="C"><strong>(C)</strong> William Francklin (विलियम फ्रैंकलिन)</div>
    <div class="pyq-opt" data-opt="D"><strong>(D)</strong> Maharishi Valmiki (महर्षि वाल्मीकि)</div>
  </div>
  <details class="pyq-details">
    <summary class="pyq-toggle-btn">👁️ Click to View Answer & Detailed 4-Option Analysis</summary>
    <div class="pyq-solution">
      <div class="pyq-ans-badge">✅ Correct Answer: <strong>(B) George Thomas (जॉर्ज थॉमस)</strong></div>
      <div class="pyq-explanation">
        <strong>🎯 Why this is the Correct Answer:</strong> Irish military adventurer George Thomas was the first to use the term <em>'Rajpootana'</em> for this region in 1800 AD. This was documented by William Francklin in 1805 in his book <em>'Military Memoirs of Mr. George Thomas'</em>.<br><br>
        <strong>🔍 Comparative Distractor Breakdown (अन्य विकल्पों का विस्तृत विश्लेषण — Why Not the Others?):</strong>
        <ul>
          <li><strong>Why not (A) Col. James Tod?</strong> Col. James Tod ('Ghode Wale Baba') first used the names <em>'Rajasthan'</em>, <em>'Raethan'</em>, and <em>'Rajwara'</em> in his 1829 landmark book <em>'Annals and Antiquities of Rajasthan'</em>.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (C) William Francklin?</strong> William Francklin did not coin the term; he merely authored the 1805 biography recording that George Thomas was the first to call this region Rajpootana.</li>
          <li><strong>Why not (D) Maharishi Valmiki?</strong> Maharishi Valmiki in the ancient Ramayana referred to the arid desert tract of Rajasthan as <em>'Marukantar' (मरुकान्तार)</em>.</li>
        </ul>
        <br><strong>💡 RPSC Memory Hook / Trap Alert:</strong> George Thomas (1800 AD) = Rajpootana; Col. Tod (1829 AD) = Rajasthan / Raethan; Valmiki = Marukantar; Vasantgarh Inscription (625 AD) = Rajasthanaditya.
      </div>
    </div>
  </details>
</div>


<div class="pyq-card" data-correct="C">
